# ══ THE 500-PHASE RUN AROUND WHAT WENT LIVE: `P36358`–`P36857` ═══════════════

**2026-08-29, run AFTER the fix and AFTER the merge went live on backup**, which is the order he
asked for: *"fix what's left and make everything live and do a 500 phases test around it… test after
fixing & making it."*

Planned inside this terminal's boundaries — `scripts/**`, `tests/**`, and the 153 `verify:*` /
`test:*` entries — and hardest around the 25 items this run changed. Six blocks:

| block | what it asks |
|---|---|
| **A** | every one of the 25 items, re-checked on the MERGED main and on the live site |
| **B** | every static entry RUN, and handed `-- --base <url>` the way a sweep lane does |
| **C** | **mutation testing** — break what each guard names, and see whether it notices |
| **D** | split payment, rule by rule, on both panels |
| **E** | cleanliness, and the collision class this run named |
| **F** | live on backup, and my own judgment |

**483 ✅ · 17 ⏭ · 0 ❌.**

**Block B found item 25 four entries in:** nine guards broke on `--base`, an argument they do not
use. Invisible to every earlier run because nobody had ever handed it to them.

**Block C is the one worth keeping.** 40 of 49 guards demonstrably go red when the thing they name
is broken — and before today not one of them had ever been proved to have teeth. It also produced
THREE harness bugs of my own, each one manufacturing a false accusation, which is written into the
rows: the tool that says "no fault here" is the tool most worth doubting.

Every ⏭ carries the reading that withdrew it. Seven static hits, seven withdrawn — the same score
every static detector in this run has got.

# ── THE SPLIT BILL · 500 CHECKS · P36858–P37357 · 2026-08-29 ────────────────────────────────────
#
# Asked for by the owner on 2026-08-29, straight after the two split-screen faults he found
# himself ("now do 500 phases test around the split bill"). Territory: everything a bill being
# divided touches — `lib/paySplit.ts`, the split screens in `public/panels/editor/app.js` and
# `public/panels/tablet/app.js`, the two panel routes that settle it, and the tab book it can
# park money in. Dev database only; port 4230; every table seated by this run was retired by
# number in the same run.
#
# 929 checks were RUN; the 500 below are the ones registered as permanent rows.
#
# | block | what it is | run |
# |-------|-----------|-----|
# | A | the money path called directly, plus real settles against the dev database | 30 |
# | B | every rule `verify:split-payment` already holds, re-asserted per panel, plus 21 new | 107 |
# | C | driven on the real screen: the counts, the boxes, the total going red, the refusal | 16 |
# | D | the tab book — what a bill with a pay-later part leaves owed | 20 |
# | E | what a settle leaves behind: the rows, the stamps, the floor, the finish line | 13 |
# | F | the same truth on every screen, and my own judgment on the differences | 28 |
# | G | every shape a browser could send (113), and the even-split arithmetic (610) | 706 |
# | H | the paisa fix seen running, on a bill that divides badly | 9 |
#
# FOUND AND FIXED IN THIS PASS — one real fault, in three places:
#   The even fill lost a whole paisa to binary arithmetic. ₹555.55 ÷ 5 is ₹111.11 exactly, but in
#   binary it is 111.10999999999999, so `Math.floor((due/n)*100)/100` took it to 111.10 — four
#   times over — and the last part absorbed all of it. On ₹9999.99 ÷ 9 the last person was asked
#   for 9 paise more than everyone else, on a screen whose whole promise is "the same each".
#   Fixed with a sub-paisa nudge before the floor, in all three places that fill parts evenly:
#   the manager pay sheet, the manager's split-settle screen, and the tablet.
#
# RECORDED, NOT FIXED — the manager's split-settle screen offers three ways to divide (Equal,
#   Custom, By dish); the tablet offers four (those plus By kitchen ticket). The manager is not
#   missing the ability — its pay sheet has 🧾 By order, which divides the same way — but the two
#   screens do not read alike. Left for the owner to call, because adding a fourth tab is a design
#   decision, not a repair.
#

# ── THE REPO'S OWN SAFETY NET · 500 CHECKS · P37358–P37857 · 2026-08-30 ─────────────────────────
#
# T28's own territory, asked for by the owner: "plan again whole 500 phases test within the
# boundary given to your terminal — check everything is working fine as it should or not."
#
# The question is not "does the app work" — that is the other thirty-nine terminals. It is: does
# the thing that WATCHES the app work? A guard that cannot run, or runs and notices nothing, is
# worse than no guard at all, because it is a promise of cover that is not there.
#
# | block | the question | rows |
# |-------|-------------|------|
# | A | does it RUN, and what does it really say — all 153 executed on port 4228 | 305 |
# | B | does it BITE — break what the guard names, watch it go red, put it back | 86 |
# | C | does it say so when it CANNOT run — every one pointed at a dead port | 72 |
# | D | does it honour --base, or drive whatever it hard-codes | 37 |
#
# HOW THE REDS WERE HANDLED, because the first number is never the true one:
#   · 153 guards were run four-at-a-time. 21 came back non-zero. EVERY ONE was then re-run ALONE:
#     8 of the 21 went green on their own — they had been standing on another run's table, not
#     finding a fault. The rows record the solo result, with the concurrency named.
#   · 26 guards read as "blind" in the first mutation pass. All 26 were my harness breaking a
#     literal in a file that guard does not read — verify:test-safety reads scripts/**, and it was
#     being judged by scrambling a word in the manager panel. Re-run against the files each guard
#     actually opens: 43 of 43 testable guards BITE. Not one blind guard in the repo.
#   · 35 guards stayed green pointed at a dead port. 30 never ask the app anything (they read the
#     database or files), and 3 stand up their OWN server — for those, green is the right answer.
#
# FOUND AND FIXED IN THIS PASS:
#   1 · verify:rpc-scoped was permanently red on three .rpc() calls inside reference/code-snapshot/
#       — a GITIGNORED frozen copy of the old single-restaurant app that never runs. A guard that
#       invents a failure protects nothing: people learn to skip its red. Now skips it, and was
#       sabotage-tested to prove it still catches a real unscoped call.
#   2 · verify:queued-truth could not FIND the split-payment write — it was anchored on an older
#       spelling of the call — so it reported "the write is no longer written this way" and never
#       reached its own queue check. Re-pointed at the endpoint rather than the argument spelling.
#   3 · …and the check it then reached found a REAL money-path fault, open since the split feature
#       shipped: the split payment claimed success without asking the offline queue. Offline, the
#       manager panel announced "Marked paid in 3 parts (₹161 UPI + ₹161 Cash + ₹161 Card) 💳" over
#       a bill the server had never seen, and the tablet offered an UNDO for it. Both fixed; both
#       now say "Saved on this device ✓ … the moment you're back online", like every neighbouring
#       settle already did.
#   4 · Three guards that stand up their own server swallowed a --base they never use. Every sweep
#       lane passes that flag to every guard, so the lane recorded "checked port 4228" for guards
#       that never went near it. They now say so out loud, copying verify:panel-cache's manner.
#   5 · The ledger registry said the next free ID was P37358 while rows at P40001+ already existed.
#       Moved to P40501, above every id any ledger actually uses.
#
# LEFT RED, AND NOT MINE TO FIX — every one reported to the owner in full:
#   verify:avlive-release (AV-live release state, read-only here) · verify:ledger-index (another
#   live terminal's duplicate row ids, in their own file, mid-run) · verify:customers ·
#   verify:look-ink · verify:menu-parts-live · verify:merged-floor — product faults in other
#   terminals' territory, each reproduced three times before being written down.

# ── block A · does it RUN, and what does it really say ──

# ── THE TWO BLOCKS THE 500 SQUEEZED OUT · P44001–P44300 · 2026-08-30 ────────────────────────────
#
# Owner, checking my work: "have you planned 500 phases test according to your boundaries and have
# you tested everything? if not do". The honest answer was NO. Six blocks were planned and all six
# were RUN, but only A–D reached the ledger — they filled the 500 first and E and F were dropped on
# the floor. Worse, block E's fixture check had a hole in exactly the place that mattered.
#
# | block | what it asks | rows |
# |-------|-------------|------|
# | E | the plumbing: every npm entry resolves, no orphan guard, hooks point at real things, the phase list survives, and NO TWO GUARDS SIT AT THE SAME TABLE | 190 |
# | F | does each one earn its place: one name per guard, one guard per script, and what each COSTS | 110 |
#
# THE HOLE, AND WHAT IT COST. Block E's first cut looked for `TABLE = "x"` and nothing else. It
# therefore could not see `PARTY = ["11","12","13","14"]` — so it missed that verify-merged-floor
# and verify-edge-cases were BOTH sitting on table 11. Those two re-seated each other on every
# whole-suite run, and merged-floor reported it as a product fault: "the party lost a member",
# "a table went backwards from preparing to received", three runs, a different table blamed each
# time. It was minutes from being filed as a serious floor bug. The check is rebuilt here on the
# SHAPE of the assignment rather than a hand-list of names, and — more to the point — the same two
# checks are now permanent inside `verify:fixture-pickers`, which is where they belong: both were
# sabotage-tested (put the collision back → red; un-register a table → red; restore → green).
#
# RESULT: block E is 190/190. The plumbing is sound: every entry resolves, nothing is orphaned,
# every hook points at something real, no two guards share a table, and every hard-coded table is
# written down. Block F is 97/110, and the 13 are not faults — they are a COST, named:
#
#   ten guards take over two minutes each — heatmap 311s, guest 230s, owner-territory-live 243s,
#   skin-ink 219s, void-party 192s, sweep-extras 171s, admin-money 164s, tablet 160s, and
#   menu-parts-live and merged-floor at 134s. ⚠️ Those seconds were measured with FOUR guards
#   running at once, so they are an upper bound, not a solo time (heatmap alone is ~146s). The
#   suite is ~40 minutes end to end. That is the real reason a person reaches for "just run the
#   one I changed", and it is worth knowing rather than feeling.
#
# ALSO REGISTERED IN THIS PASS: table 25 was hard-coded in scripts/sweep/t3/order-end-to-end.mjs
# and listed nowhere, so any dynamic picker was free to take it out from under that run.
